Visual witness description of PDF page 12 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two-panel schematic diagram. Left panel titled "Fixed isolated down strand": vertical time axis $t$ directed upwards with ticks $t_0$ and $t_1$; three vertical lines representing slot coordinates; a highlighted strand in red traversing between slots via horizontal segments (instantaneous exchanges), with a vertical segment between $t_0$ and $t_1$ labeled $X$; text below: "The incident holding intervals contain no exchange marks." Right panel titled "Recolor, delete, and fill": three vertical slot lines with solid and dashed segments; top endpoint flip with curved arrow labeled "up $\to$ down"; bottom endpoint flip with curved arrow labeled "down $\to$ up"; text below: "New incident marks exchange two up colors."
```


In particular, <a href="#eq:isolateddomination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:isolateddomination">[eq:isolateddomination]</a> bounds a joint event, not the strand law conditioned on isolation. This is precisely what is needed when excluding rare isolated strands with large displacement.

<a id="stmt-prop-good"></a>
<div id="prop:good" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:goodprob"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Good exposures). *Under $\mathbb P_P$ there is an event $\mathcal G$ with $$\label{eq:goodprob}
 \mathbb P_P(\mathcal G^c)=o(\beta^{-5/2})$$ having the following properties in $B_{12R_*}(x)$, in either direction of traversal.*

1.  *A strand makes at most $c_0L$ inter-site steps in a block and at most $C\log\beta$ in a bin. The bounds also apply to segments traced from an intermediate visit to the region, in either time direction.*

2.  *At each block cut, every ball of radius $c_1L$ centered in the region contains at most five obstacle slots. Local regularity at radius $R_*$ about $x$ holds at all times. In every block, the set of fully regular times at that radius has length at least half the block.*

3.  *A strand is called isolated in a block and direction if no other obstacle slot lies within $c_2L$ of its starting site. Every such strand stays at distance greater than two from all other obstacles and has maximal site displacement at most $CL^{3/4}$ in the block.*

*The constants are fixed in the order $c_0$, then $c_2$, then $c_1$, with each sufficiently large for the preceding choices. The event and all its properties repeat on subsequent copies of the period.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-good"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-good</summary>

<a id="eq:marklist"></a>
*Proof.* First control the number of strand steps. A chronological connected list of $j$ inter-site marks in an interval of length $u$, anchored at a prescribed site, has probability at most $$\label{eq:marklist}
 \frac{(Cu)^j}{j!}.$$ Indeed, there are at most $C^j$ successive site and slot-edge choices, and the ordered time simplex has volume $u^j/j!$; apply the factorial bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:loopinputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:loopinputs">3</a>. The same count works in reverse order. It bounds lists anywhere in the interval, so it also covers a segment beginning at an arbitrary intermediate visit; no union over real starting times is necessary. Taking $j=c_0L$ on blocks, with $c_0$ large, gives $e^{-cL}$ tails. Taking $j=C\log\beta$ on bins gives rapidly small tails by the factorial in <a href="#eq:marklist" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marklist">[eq:marklist]</a>. A polynomial union over sites, blocks, bins, and both directions proves the first property with rapidly small error. For this count and the next one enlarge the region to $B_{20R_*}(x)$.

At a fixed block cut and center, six obstacles in a ball of radius $c_1L$ supply six distinct slot points there. There are at most $CL^{18}$ such six-tuples. Proposition <a href="#prop:snapshot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:snapshot">8</a>, followed by a union over centers and at most $C\beta$ cuts, bounds this failure probability by <a id="eq:sixfailure"></a>$$\label{eq:sixfailure}
 C\beta R_*^3L^{18}\lambda^6
       \le C\beta^{-3+18\delta}
       =C\beta^{-2.91}=o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ Seam permutations preserve the site counts, including multiplicities, so this estimate holds on either seam side.

If six blocked sites were present in one of the required fixed-radius balls during a block, one obstacle at each site could be traced back to the block cut. The first property would place their six distinct starting slots in a ball of radius $c_1L$, once $c_1$ is large enough. This is impossible. Thus local regularity holds at all times. For the remaining condition, Proposition <a href="#prop:geometryinputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:geometryinputs">7</a> bounds the expected measure of times when distant-component regularity fails in any block $I$ by $C|I|e^{-cR_*^\xi}$. Markov’s inequality and a union over blocks show, with rapidly small error, that it fails on less than half of every block. This proves the second property.

The step bound also ensures that an isolated strand cannot meet another obstacle during its block. Any potential encounter can be traced back in both strands to their distinct block-start slots, at total distance at most a fixed multiple of $c_0L$. Choose $c_2$ larger than that multiple. This argument also covers strands entering the region, by tracing backward from the encounter.

It remains to bound the isolated strand’s displacement. The site coordinate of the base walk in Lemma <a href="#lem:isolatedpath" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:isolatedpath">9</a> jumps at rate $S$ to each nearest neighbor. Exponential martingales in each coordinate give, for $u\le2L$, $$\mathbb P_{\mathrm b}\left(\max_{0\le v\le u}
                   |X(v)-X(0)|_\infty>CL^{3/4}\right)
       \le C e^{-c\sqrt L}.$$ For example, use an exponential parameter of order $L^{-1/4}$; the martingale compensator is at most a constant times $uL^{-1/2}$. On the step event already established, strands beginning in the region stay interior. Lemma <a href="#lem:isolatedpath" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:isolatedpath">9</a> therefore bounds the joint event of isolation and excessive displacement. A polynomial union over slots, blocks, and directions is rapidly small. Seams make no spatial displacement; for a seam block, enumerate also the slots on its other seam side. Combining these exceptional sets proves <a href="#eq:goodprob" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:goodprob">[eq:goodprob]</a> and the third property. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Heat propagation at a fixed good exposure

From this point, all actual kernels kill on $E$, not at $i$. The estimates in this subsection are deterministic statements about the fresh walk at an exposure in $\mathcal G$. The earlier picture probabilities have already done their work in constructing that event.

<a id="stmt-lem-heatbound"></a>
<div id="lem:heatbound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heatbound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Local heat bound). *On $\mathcal G$, a fresh-walk interval kernel of elapsed length $u$, additionally killed on exit from $B_{R_*}(x)$, has maximum entry at most $$\label{eq:heatbound}
 C(1+u)^{-3/2}.$$ The bound holds for either time direction, for every starting slice, with additional spatial killing, and across copies of the period.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heatbound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heatbound</summary>

*Proof.* We use Nash’s energy-dissipation argument <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Nash1958](#ref-Nash1958), pp. 935–936]. For a nonnegative input of mass at most one, let $l=\lVert v\rVert_2^2$. The all-time local regularity in Proposition <a href="#prop:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good">10</a> permits <a href="#eq:nash" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nash">[eq:nash]</a> at every smooth time, so $l'\le-cl^{5/3}$. Instantaneous operations are contractions by Lemma <a href="#lem:kernelenergy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernelenergy">4</a>. Integrating gives $\lVert v\rVert_2\le C(1+u)^{-3/4}$ for a unit point input. Split the kernel at its midpoint and express an entry as the inner product of a forward mass and an adjoint mass, each propagated for $u/2$. Their two norm bounds give <a href="#eq:heatbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatbound">[eq:heatbound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-localization"></a>
<div id="lem:localization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Displacement of the fresh walk). *On $\mathcal G$, from any slot above $B_{6R}(x)$ and any starting slice, a fresh walk run for elapsed time $u\le2\beta$ in either direction stays within site distance <a id="eq:localization"></a>$$\label{eq:localization}
 C(1+u/L)L^{3/4}$$ of its starting site, apart from a rapidly small probability uniform over the fixed good exposure. The assertion bounds the probability of excessive displacement before killing. In particular, adding killing on exit from $B_{R_*}(x)$ changes these propagations by a rapidly small amount in total mass.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-localization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-localization</summary>

*Proof.* Consider first a piece of one block, of length at most $2L$, starting at any slice and over a site $z$ in $B_{9R}(x)$. Stop on exiting $B_L(z)$, including its exit step in the displacement estimate. Every obstacle strand visiting this ball or its immediate neighbors traces back at most $c_0L$ steps to a distinct slot at the deterministic block cut. The obstacle count in Proposition <a href="#prop:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good">10</a> thus shows that at most five strands can be relevant before stopping. Their instantaneous neighborhoods have uniformly bounded size.

The rate jumps of each site coordinate have bounded rates and unit increments. Their drift vanishes away from the neighboring obstacles and is uniformly bounded elsewhere. Lemma <a href="#lem:heatbound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heatbound">11</a>, with the stopping-ball killing, bounds the expected occupation time near the relevant strands by $$C\int_0^{2L}(1+t)^{-3/2}\,dt\le C.$$ The expectation is uniform in the starting slot and slice, including either side of any prescribed operation.

A forced transport occurs only when the fresh walk is at its prescribed departure hole. In any bin there are at most $C\log\beta$ relevant transports, by the bin step bound for the five strands. Apply <a href="#eq:heatbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatbound">[eq:heatbound]</a> to the probability of presence just before each such operation. Summing over bins, using their lengths between one and three, bounds the expected number of forced steps before stopping by $$C\log\beta\sum_{n\ge0}(1+n)^{-3/2}\le C\log\beta.$$ The same estimate holds from an arbitrary intermediate slice: a unit-time interval intersects only a bounded number of the original bins. These estimates use deterministic transport times at fixed exposure, with no conditional Poisson assertion.

Let $A$ be the accumulated occupation time near obstacles plus the number of forced steps. From every stopping slice and surviving state, the conditional expected remaining accumulation is at most $C\log\beta$. The Markov property and Markov’s inequality show that each further increment of size $2C\log\beta+1$ has conditional probability at most $1/2$. Occupation time is continuous and the transport-count overshoot is at most one. Restarting at successive thresholds therefore gives, for each fixed $a>0$, $$\Pr(A>aL^{3/4})\le C_a
        \exp\{-c_aL^{3/4}/\log\beta\}.$$ This controls both the integrated drift and the forced displacement.

Subtract the integrated drift from the ordinary rate jumps. For each coordinate, bounded rates and unit jumps imply that its compensated process $M_t$ has $\exp\{\theta M_t-C\theta^2t\}$ as a supermartingale for $|\theta|\le1$, with a fixed $C$. The maximal inequality with $\theta$ of order $L^{-1/4}$ gives $$\Pr\bigl(\max_{t\le2L}|M_t|>aL^{3/4}\bigr)
                                  \le C_a e^{-c_a\sqrt L}.$$ The three displacement contributions prove the claimed bound on one block piece. Since $CL^{3/4}<L$ for large $\beta$, they also bound the probability of reaching the stopping sphere. Seam operations change no site coordinate.

An interval of length $u$ meets at most $C(1+u/L)$ block pieces. Apply the preceding uniform bound successively and add their displacements. There are polynomially many pieces, so the total failure probability remains rapidly small. Throughout an interval of length at most $2\beta$, the resulting displacement is $O(\beta L^{-1/4})=o(R)$. The walk therefore stays in the local region used above, and in $B_{R_*}(x)$. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Overlaps of flows

The final geometric estimate measures the energy cost of superposing flows from different source locations. It retains their separation, which will be essential when averaging the sparse source locations.

<a id="stmt-lem-flowoverlap"></a>
<div id="lem:flowoverlap" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:flowoverlap"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Flow overlap). *At a fully regular slice on $\mathcal G$, choose the unit flows in <a href="#eq:flowenvelopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flowenvelopes">[eq:flowenvelopes]</a> for available slots $a,a'$ over $B_{6R}(x)$. Then $$\label{eq:flowoverlap}
 \sum_e|J_a(e)J_{a'}(e)|
       \le\frac{C}{1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(a,a')},$$ where the distance is between their sites.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flowoverlap"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flowoverlap</summary>

*Proof.* Where both edge distances from the source sites are at most $R_*$, use the two inverse-square envelopes. Their convolution in dimension three is bounded by $C/(1+b)$ at source separation $b$: balls of radius $b/2$ about either source contribute $C/(1+b)$, and dyadic shells outside them have the same total bound.

If one source distance exceeds $R_*$, both are at least a fixed multiple of $R_*$, since $a,a'$ lie over $B_{6R}(x)$ and $R=o(R_*)$. The global envelopes then bound the remaining sum by $$C R_*^{3-2(199/100)}
       =C R_*^{-98/100}
       \le C R^{-1}.$$ The last inequality follows from $(5/3)(98/100)=49/30>3/2+\delta/4$. As $b\le12R$, this is bounded by the right-hand side of <a href="#eq:flowoverlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flowoverlap">[eq:flowoverlap]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:profiles"></a>
### The ideal potential and its profiles

We now choose the trial vector for the capacity comparison. The free potential includes every positive number of periods, so its capacity already contains the full ideal-magnon density. A spatial cutoff makes it a finite-volume test vector without truncating those periods. We also obtain the spatial and temporal estimates needed to compare its evolution with the walk among holes.

Fix $S$, put $\ell=2S$, and use the scales in <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a>. Let $E=D_0\cup F$, where $F$ is a finite deterministic set of slot-time pins, and let $i=(x,a)$ be an unpinned post-seam slot at time zero. Throughout the comparison assume that $B_{50R_*}(x)$ lies in the interior of the cube and that no point of $E$ lies above this ball. The picture law is $\mathbb P_P$, where $P=E\cup\{i\}$. At a fixed exposure let $U$ be the forward one-period kernel killed on $E$, retaining its zero rows and columns on killed cut slots. Set $A=I-U$ and use the energy $e_y$ of Section <a href="#sec:capacity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:capacity">3</a>.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The all-turn free potential

<a id="eq:slotdecomposition"></a>
On $\mathbb Z^3\times\{1,\ldots,\ell\}$, let $\mathscr L$ have rate $1/2$ on every slot edge, and let $T_s=\exp(s\mathscr L)$. Let $J$ be the projection that averages the slots at each site. For a slot-uniform vector the site jump rate is $\ell/2=S$ to each neighbor. Denote this site semigroup by $P_s$, and its kernel from the origin by $p_s(z)$. Its Fourier multiplier is <a id="eq:freemultiplier"></a>$$\label{eq:freemultiplier}
 \widehat p_s(k)
 =\exp\!\left[-2sS\sum_{j=1}^3(1-\cos k_j)\right],
 \qquad k\in[-\pi,\pi]^3.$$ If a vector has zero sum on the slots at every site, then $\mathscr L$ acts on it as $-3\ell I$. Therefore $$\label{eq:slotdecomposition}
 T_s=P_sJ+e^{-3\ell s}(I-J).$$ The free seam is $J$. It commutes with $T_s$, so the ideal full-period operator, in either direction, is $$U_0=JT_\beta=T_\beta J=P_\beta J,
 \qquad
 (U_0^n)_{(z,b),(x,a)}=\frac1\ell p_{n\beta}(z-x)
 \quad(n\ge1).$$ In particular the slot modes removed by the seam contribute nothing to a positive full turn.

<a id="eq:freepotential"></a>
<a id="eq:freepotentialidentity"></a>
<a id="eq:trialvector"></a>
With $\eta=\mathbf 1_{\{i\}}$ on the full slot lattice, define the slot-uniform function $$\label{eq:freepotential}
 v(z)=\frac1\ell\sum_{n\ge1}p_{n\beta}(z-x),
 \qquad c=\frac1{1+v(x)}.$$ The estimates below show that the sum is finite and $v(x)=O_S(\beta^{-3/2})$. Positivity and the semigroup property give $$\label{eq:freepotentialidentity}
 U_0(\eta+v)=v.$$ Choose $\chi:\mathbb R^3\to[0,1]$ smooth, equal to one when $|z-x|_\infty\le R/2$ and zero when $|z-x|_\infty\ge R$, with first and second derivatives bounded by $C/R$ and $C/R^2$. Use its restriction to sites as a slot-uniform multiplier. The full trial vector is $$\label{eq:trialvector}
 h=c\chi(\eta+v),$$ and the same symbol denotes its restriction to the cut holes. It vanishes on every killed cut slot and satisfies $h(i)=1$. Moreover $p_s(z)\le p_s(0)$, by the Fourier representation, so $0\le h\le1$.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Forward and reverse profiles

Put $g=c(\eta+v)$ on the full slot lattice. The forward and reverse profiles, before restriction to holes, are <a id="eq:forwardprofile"></a><a id="eq:reverseprofile"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 a_s&=\chi T_s g\quad(0\le s<\beta),
 &a_\beta&=\chi JT_\beta g,                         \label{eq:forwardprofile}\\
 b_0&=\chi g,\qquad b_{0+}=\chi Jg,
 &b_s&=\chi T_sJg\quad(0<s\le\beta).                 \label{eq:reverseprofile}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $s$ is elapsed time in the indicated direction. The forward traversal contains the seam at its end. The reverse traversal contains it at its beginning, with $b_0$ its incoming value and $b_{0+}$ its outgoing value. Each traversal includes the seam exactly once. At every slice, the same symbols also denote the restrictions to the holes on the specified side of any instantaneous operation; “full profile values” will always refer to the formulas before restriction.

Multiplication by $\chi$ commutes with $J$. Thus the full profiles undergo the ideal seam operation, and <a href="#eq:freepotentialidentity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:freepotentialidentity">[eq:freepotentialidentity]</a> gives the endpoint identities <a id="eq:profileendpoints"></a>$$\label{eq:profileendpoints}
 a_0=b_0=h,\qquad a_\beta=b_\beta=h-c\eta.$$ The smooth part of either profile is the contribution of $cv$; the point part is the contribution of $c\eta$. This decomposition also applies to full profile values.

For $r_z=|z-x|_\infty$ and $d\ge1$, introduce <a id="eq:weights"></a>$$\label{eq:weights}
 \begin{split}
 G(z)&=\beta^{-1}(D_*+r_z)^{-1},\qquad
 W(z)=\beta^{-1}(D_*+r_z)^{-2},\\
 P_d(z)&=d^{-3}(1+r_z/d)^{-12},\qquad
 W_d(z)=d^{-4}(1+r_z/d)^{-12}.
 \end{split}$$ For a slot function $u$, a bounded-distance difference at $z$ means $u(z,a)-u(z',a')$ with $|z-z'|_\infty$ at most a fixed constant; the constants below may depend on that constant. This includes differences between two slots at the same site.

<a id="stmt-lem-freebounds"></a>
<div id="lem:freebounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:smoothbounds"></a>
<a id="eq:pointbounds"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Bounds for the free profiles). *For fixed $S$ and all sufficiently large $\beta$, the following bounds hold uniformly in the direction, elapsed time, site, and slot. For the full smooth profile $u_s$, its absolute value, bounded-distance differences, and open-interval time derivative are bounded, respectively, by $$\label{eq:smoothbounds}
 C G(z),\qquad C W(z),\qquad C W(z)/D_*.$$ For the full point profile $w_s$, put $d=\sqrt{1+s}$. The corresponding bounds are $$\label{eq:pointbounds}
 C P_d(z),\qquad C W_d(z),\qquad C W_d(z)/d.$$ The smooth profile is slot-uniform and does not change at the seam. The slot differences and seam changes of the point profile satisfy the difference bound in <a href="#eq:pointbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pointbounds">[eq:pointbounds]</a>. For each fixed $a>0$, when $s\ge a\beta$ the bounds <a href="#eq:smoothbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothbounds">[eq:smoothbounds]</a> also hold for $w_s$, with a constant depending on $a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-freebounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-freebounds</summary>

<a id="eq:heatderivative"></a>
*Proof.* We first record the heat-kernel estimates before applying the cutoff. For every fixed integer $m\ge0$ and $j=0,1,2$, Fourier inversion gives $$\label{eq:heatderivative}
 |\nabla^j p_t(z)|
 \le C_{m,j}(1+t)^{-(3+j)/2}
       \left(1+\frac{|z|_\infty}{\sqrt{1+t}}\right)^{-m}.$$ Here $j$ nearest-neighbor differences may be taken in arbitrary coordinate directions. One time derivative satisfies the same bound with $j=2$. To verify the estimates, write $\omega(k)=2S\sum_j(1-\cos k_j)$ and use $\omega(k)\ge c_S|k|^2$ on $[-\pi,\pi]^3$. A $j$-fold difference inserts a multiplier vanishing to order $j$ at zero; a time derivative inserts $-\omega$, which vanishes to order two. The integral without frequency differentiation is thus $O((1+t)^{-(3+j)/2})$. For the spatial weight, integrate by parts $m$ times in a coordinate for which $|z_j|=|z|_\infty$. The integral of the absolute value of the differentiated multiplier is at most $C_{m,j}(1+t)^{(m-3-j)/2}$: near zero this follows by rescaling $k$ by $(1+t)^{-1/2}$, using $\nabla\omega=O(|k|)$ and bounded higher derivatives; away from zero the exponential dominates every power of $t$. The periodic integrands have no boundary terms. Combining this bound with the bound without integration by parts proves <a href="#eq:heatderivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatderivative">[eq:heatderivative]</a>. The case $0\le t\le1$ follows from uniform bounds on the same derivatives.

<a id="eq:heatsum"></a>
The slot-uniform part of the smooth evolution is a sum of site kernels at times $n\beta+s$, $n\ge1$. For $j=0,1,2$, choose $m>j+1$ in <a href="#eq:heatderivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatderivative">[eq:heatderivative]</a>. Splitting the time sum at $n\beta+s$ of order $r_z^2$, or comparing it with its integral on dyadic intervals, gives $$\label{eq:heatsum}
 \sum_{n\ge1}(n\beta+s)^{-(3+j)/2}
     \left(1+\frac{r_z}{\sqrt{n\beta+s}}\right)^{-m}
 \le \frac{C}{\beta}(D_*+r_z)^{-1-j}.$$ For completeness, when $r_z\le D_*$ the sum without its spatial factor is $O(\beta^{-(3+j)/2})$. When $r_z>D_*$, the terms with $n\beta+s\ge r_z^2$ sum to $C\beta^{-1}r_z^{-1-j}$. For the remaining terms, use the bound $(1+r_z/\sqrt t)^{-m}\le(\sqrt t/r_z)^m$ and sum $t^{(m-3-j)/2}$ up to $r_z^2$; the same bound results. This proves <a href="#eq:heatsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatsum">[eq:heatsum]</a> and the asserted pointwise convergence of $v$. It gives the value and difference estimates in <a href="#eq:smoothbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothbounds">[eq:smoothbounds]</a>; for time derivatives use $$\beta^{-1}(D_*+r_z)^{-3}\le W(z)/D_*.$$

For the point part, <a href="#eq:heatderivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatderivative">[eq:heatderivative]</a> gives <a href="#eq:pointbounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pointbounds">[eq:pointbounds]</a> with $m=12$. Formula <a href="#eq:slotdecomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slotdecomposition">[eq:slotdecomposition]</a> adds only a site-supported slot deviation decaying as $e^{-3\ell s}$. This deviation, its spatial differences, and its time derivative satisfy the same bounds, with constants depending on $S$. In the reverse direction the initial seam removes that deviation; at a seam its change is bounded by the incoming absolute value of the slot deviation and hence by $C W_d(z)$. The smooth part is unchanged because it is slot-uniform.

It remains to justify multiplication by $\chi$. Value and time derivative estimates are preserved. In a spatial difference the extra term contains a difference of $\chi$, supported where $r_z$ is of order $R$. There $G(z)/R\le C W(z)$, and $P_d(z)/R\le C W_d(z)$ since $d\le\sqrt{1+\beta}\ll R$. Thus the difference estimates are preserved as well. Finally, if $s\ge a\beta$, then $d$ is comparable with $D_*$; direct comparison of the envelopes gives $P_d\le C_aG$, $W_d\le C_aW$, and $W_d/d\le C_aW/D_*$. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:freeidealdensity"></a>
At the source site, the free potential therefore recovers the ideal density exactly. Fourier inversion and monotone convergence give $$\label{eq:freeidealdensity}
 \begin{split}
 \ell v(x)=\sum_{n\ge1}p_{n\beta}(0)
 &=\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^3}\frac{d^3k}{(2\pi)^3}
      \sum_{n\ge1}e^{-2n\beta S\sum_j(1-\cos k_j)}\\
 &=n_S^{\rm sw}(\beta).
 \end{split}$$ The integral is finite in dimension three, also as a consequence of the heat-kernel sum in <a href="#eq:heatsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heatsum">[eq:heatsum]</a>.

The uncut potential need not be square summable. Its evolutions above are defined pointwise by the convergent positive series, while the trial vector and every restricted profile used in a finite cube have finite support. Equation <a href="#eq:freeidealdensity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:freeidealdensity">[eq:freeidealdensity]</a> identifies $v(x)$ with the target in Proposition <a href="#prop:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparison">5</a>. It remains to bound the change of return capacity when the ideal period is replaced by the walk among holes. Define $\mu_+,\mu_-$ from this $h$ and $c$ by <a href="#eq:capacityresiduals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capacityresiduals">[eq:capacityresiduals]</a>. Section <a href="#sec:sources" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sources">6</a> realizes these residuals as propagated local errors of the profiles.

<a id="sec:sources"></a>
### Comparison sources and their cancellation

We now express the errors of the trial potential as sources in the actual heat equation. The key fact is a conservation identity: along an isolated obstacle, the signed source is an endpoint difference of the profile, up to its cutoff commutator. Its size is therefore controlled by the obstacle’s displacement, although the total variation of the source counts every jump. We establish this identity before using it to estimate either the scalar or the energy pairing.

Throughout this section we impose the interiority and absence of pins over $B_{50R_*}(x)$ required in Section <a href="#sec:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profiles">5</a>. Constants are uniform in the finite cube, the allowed pin set, and the injection slot. The two traversals and their profiles are those defined there: $a_s$ is the forward profile and $b_s$ is the reverse profile, each indexed by its own elapsed time $s\in[0,\beta]$. Profiles are evaluated on the full slot lattice before restriction to the current holes.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Sources on intervals and at operations

<a id="eq:continuous-source"></a>
<a id="eq:atomic-source"></a>
Fix one of the two directions and write $\varphi_s$ for its profile. On an open interval containing no instantaneous operation, let $\mathscr L_s^{\mathrm{act}}$ be the generator on the holes, with the prescribed killing. The continuous source is the vector $$\label{eq:continuous-source}
 \mathscr L_s^{\mathrm{act}}(\varphi_s|_{H_s})
             -(\partial_s\varphi_s)|_{H_s}.$$ Here $H_s$ denotes the hole set on the slice. At an instantaneous transition $K:\mathbb R^{H_-}\to\mathbb R^{H_+}$, the atomic source, on the outgoing slice, is $$\label{eq:atomic-source}
 K(\varphi_-|_{H_-})-\varphi_+|_{H_+}.$$ In reverse time these are the transposed maps, in reverse order. Thus a reverse atomic source lies before the operation when viewed in forward time.

Write $s^+$ and $s^-$ for these collections of continuous densities and atomic vectors in the forward and reverse traversals. Integrating against a collection will mean a Lebesgue integral of its continuous part plus the sum over its atoms. Every source is supported above $B_{R+2}(x)$. There are no pins of $E$ or boundary slots in this region, so killing creates no additional source there.

<a id="eq:backgroundsource"></a>
<a id="eq:backgroundbound"></a>
There are two types of source. The first is the cutoff commutator, defined on the full lattice by $$\label{eq:backgroundsource}
 \gamma_s=\mathscr L\varphi_s-\partial_s\varphi_s,
 \qquad b_*:=\beta^{-1}R^{-3}.$$ The uncut profile solves the free heat equation. Consequently $\gamma_s$ is supported where $R/3\le r_z\le2R$, and $$\label{eq:backgroundbound}
 |\gamma_s(z,a)|\le Cb_*.$$ These assertions hold separately for the smooth part and the point part of either profile. Indeed the commutator of $\mathscr L$ with $\chi$ consists of a cutoff second difference times a profile value, and products of first differences. On this annulus, Lemma <a href="#lem:freebounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:freebounds">14</a> gives values at most $C/(\beta R)$ and first differences at most $C/(\beta R^2)$, uniformly in elapsed time. The bounds on the cutoff derivatives prove <a href="#eq:backgroundbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:backgroundbound">[eq:backgroundbound]</a>. There is no cutoff atom at the seam, because the sitewise cutoff commutes with both full-profile seam averaging and restriction within a site.

<a id="eq:removed-edge-source"></a>
<a id="eq:transport-source"></a>
The remaining sources come from obstacles. A removed edge from a hole $j$ to an obstacle $k$ contributes $$\label{eq:removed-edge-source}
 \tfrac12(\varphi_s(j)-\varphi_s(k))
 \quad\text{at the hole }j.$$ If an obstacle moves from $k$ to a hole $j$, the hole moves from $j$ to $k$. The source on this arriving hole is $$\label{eq:transport-source}
 \varphi_s(j)-\varphi_s(k).$$ Exchanges between two obstacles create no source. At a seam the source is the difference between the actual average on the remaining hole slots and the full-profile average, restricted to the outgoing holes. It vanishes at sites with no obstacles.

Assign a removed-edge source to its obstacle and a transport source to the obstacle responsible for the transport. Assign seam sources at a site to its obstacle strands, in any fixed manner. If a strand is isolated, it is the only obstacle there and receives the entire seam source at that site. These assignments can be made either in a block or in a bin, with strands enumerated at its initial slice in the direction being used. On $\mathcal G$, tracing a strand back from a source site in $B_{R+2}(x)$ uses at most $CL$ steps in its block, by Proposition <a href="#prop:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good">10</a>. Thus every strand contributing a nonzero source starts in $B_{4R}(x)$, for large $\beta$. All subsequent strand sums are restricted to this ball.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Conservation along an isolated strand

<a id="stmt-lem-signedsource"></a>
<div id="lem:signedsource" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:signedsource"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Signed source identity). *Fix a good picture in $\mathcal G$ and one directed block $I=[u,v]$. Let $Z_s$ be an isolated obstacle strand starting above $B_{4R}(x)$ on this block, with its specified sides at operations. For either profile part, the total signed mass of the assigned obstacle sources, obtained by summing over source slots and integrating in time, is $$\label{eq:signedsource}
 \varphi_v(Z_v)-\varphi_u(Z_u)
                  +\int_u^v\gamma_s(Z_s)\,ds.$$ The endpoint values include any seam assigned to the block. The same formula holds in reverse elapsed time.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-signedsource"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-signedsource</summary>

*Proof.* At every smooth time, all neighboring slots of $Z_s$ are holes, by isolation. Summing <a href="#eq:removed-edge-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:removed-edge-source">[eq:removed-edge-source]</a> over these slots therefore gives $$\frac12\sum_{j\sim Z_s}
             (\varphi_s(j)-\varphi_s(Z_s))
 = (\mathscr L\varphi_s)(Z_s)
 = (\partial_s\varphi_s)(Z_s)+\gamma_s(Z_s).$$ The transport contribution <a href="#eq:transport-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-source">[eq:transport-source]</a> is exactly the profile increment along each jump of $Z_s$.

It remains to check the seam, where the two hole sets can differ. At the strand’s site let $H_-,H_+$ be the incoming and outgoing holes, and let $Z_-,Z_+$ be the corresponding obstacle slots. The actual hole map preserves the sum over holes. The full-profile seam preserves the sum over all slots. Since the obstacle is the only one at this site, the total seam source is exactly $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{H_+}(K\varphi_- -\varphi_+)
 &=\sum_{H_-}\varphi_- -\sum_{H_+}\varphi_+\\
 &=\varphi_+(Z_+)-\varphi_-(Z_-).
\end{aligned}$$ Thus it also supplies the increment along the obstacle strand. Integrating the time derivatives and summing all increments telescopes to <a href="#eq:signedsource" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:signedsource">[eq:signedsource]</a>. Reverse traversal has the same conservation of counting sums and uses the inverse transport, so this proof applies with reverse elapsed time as well. ◻

</details>

We next turn this identity into bounds that retain both the signed mass and the total variation. Keeping the two quantities separate is essential: total variation controls propagation and localization, whereas the smaller signed mass controls long spatial scales.

<a id="stmt-lem-cancellation"></a>
<div id="lem:cancellation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:blocksourcevariation"></a>
<a id="eq:blocksourcecancellation"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Block source bounds). *On a picture in $\mathcal G$, let a strand start above $z\in B_{4R}(x)$ in a directed block of length between $L$ and $2L$. For the smooth profile part, take $\mathcal W=W$. For a point-profile part, suppose that $d=\sqrt{1+s}$ is comparable throughout the block and $\inf d\ge L$, and take $\mathcal W=W_d$ at its starting elapsed time. Then the obstacle sources assigned to this strand are supported within distance $CL$ of $z$ and have total variation at most $$\label{eq:blocksourcevariation}
 CL\mathcal W(z).$$ If the strand is isolated, their signed sum has absolute value at most $$\label{eq:blocksourcecancellation}
 CL^{3/4}\mathcal W(z).$$ Both assertions also hold with $\mathcal W=W$ for the point profile on blocks in the second half of elapsed time, and for the sum of that part and the smooth part, after changing the constant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cancellation</summary>

*Proof.* The step bounds in Proposition <a href="#prop:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good">10</a> keep the strand and its adjacent source sites within $CL$ of its starting site. The weights $W$ are comparable throughout this region, because $L\ll D_*$. The stated hypotheses give the same comparability for $W_d$. Each strand has bounded degree, contributes for at most $2L$ units of continuous time, and makes at most $CL$ jumps. The difference bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:freebounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:freebounds">14</a>, together with <a href="#eq:removed-edge-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:removed-edge-source">[eq:removed-edge-source]</a> and <a href="#eq:transport-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-source">[eq:transport-source]</a>, give <a href="#eq:blocksourcevariation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:blocksourcevariation">[eq:blocksourcevariation]</a>. At a seam, fixed slot multiplicity bounds the number of terms, and the difference of the two averages is bounded by the slot-difference bound. This adds at most $C\mathcal W(z)$.

For an isolated strand use Lemma <a href="#lem:signedsource" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:signedsource">15</a>. Its endpoint displacement is at most $CL^{3/4}$. The spatial endpoint difference therefore costs at most $CL^{3/4}W(z)$ in the smooth case. Time variation costs $CLW(z)/D_*$, and a seam increment costs at most $CW(z)$. On the cutoff annulus, $b_*\le CW(z)/D_*$, so the integral in <a href="#eq:signedsource" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:signedsource">[eq:signedsource]</a> has the same time bound. Since $1+L/D_*\le CL^{3/4}$, this proves <a href="#eq:blocksourcecancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:blocksourcecancellation">[eq:blocksourcecancellation]</a> for the smooth part.

For the point part the time derivative is bounded by $CW_d/d$. On the cutoff annulus its commutator is bounded by $$C\bigl(W_d/R+P_d/R^2\bigr)\le CW_d/d,$$ because $d\le\sqrt{1+\beta}\ll R$. The same endpoint argument gives $C[L^{3/4}+1+L/d]W_d(z)$, which proves the stated bound. Finally Lemma <a href="#lem:freebounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:freebounds">14</a> bounds the point part by the smooth envelopes when $s\ge\beta/2$. Addition proves the last assertion; the division at $\beta/2$ is a block endpoint. ◻

</details>

Near elapsed time zero, $W_d$ varies too quickly to use block cancellation. The bins of length between $1$ and $3$ provide the following uniform replacement. We write $H_\beta=C(1+\log\beta)^C$ for a fixed power of a logarithm; the constants, and hence this power, may be enlarged at subsequent uses.

<a id="stmt-lem-sourcebins"></a>
<div id="lem:sourcebins" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Bin source bounds). *On a picture in $\mathcal G$, the obstacle sources assigned to one strand in one directed bin, starting above $z\in B_{4R}(x)$ at elapsed time $s$, have total variation at most <a id="eq:binsourcevariation"></a>$$\label{eq:binsourcevariation}
 H_\beta\bigl(W(z)+W_d(z)\bigr),\qquad d=\sqrt{1+s}.$$ The two profile parts separately satisfy this bound with their own weight. Their source sites lie within $C\log\beta$ of $z$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sourcebins"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sourcebins</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#prop:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good">10</a> bounds both displacement and the number of jumps in a bin by $C\log\beta$. Continuous contributions have bounded time length. Within a bin, $1+s$ changes by a bounded factor, while a spatial shift of $C\log\beta$ changes any of the polynomial envelopes in Lemma <a href="#lem:freebounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:freebounds">14</a> by at most a fixed power of $1+\log\beta$. The formulas for removed edges, transports, and seam averaging now give the result. ◻

</details>

There are at most $CR^3$ possible starting slots in each bin and $C\beta$ bins, while $W+W_d\le C$. Including the background, the total variation of either complete source collection on $\mathcal G$ is therefore at most $CH_\beta\beta R^3$. In particular, multiplying a rapidly small propagation error by one or two such total variations still gives a rapidly small error.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Propagation and the two-source identity

<a id="eq:residualdefinition"></a>
Let $T_{v,u}$ denote actual forward propagation between specified slices, including exactly the operations lying between them. Reverse propagation is the adjoint with the ordered endpoints. Propagate every source from its insertion slice to its terminal cut in its own direction. The resulting signed masses are precisely the residuals defined in Section <a href="#sec:capacity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:capacity">3</a>: $$\label{eq:residualdefinition}
 \mu_+=\int T_{\beta,s}\,s^+(ds)=Uh-h+c\eta,
 \qquad
 \mu_- =\int T^{\mathrm{rev}}_{\beta,s}\,s^-(ds)
                         =U^*h-h+c\eta.$$ For example, on a smooth interval the difference between the actual mass and the profile satisfies the actual heat equation with forcing <a href="#eq:continuous-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuous-source">[eq:continuous-source]</a>. At an operation it is multiplied by the actual map and then receives <a href="#eq:atomic-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atomic-source">[eq:atomic-source]</a>. Telescoping from the initial profile $h$ to the terminal profile $h-c\eta$ proves <a href="#eq:residualdefinition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residualdefinition">[eq:residualdefinition]</a>.

For a scalar pairing it is useful to make this substitution twice. Read the reverse profile at forward physical time $t$ by writing $\bar b_t=b_{\beta-t}$, always on the specified side of an operation. In particular it is evaluated on the outgoing slice when paired with a forward source.

<a id="stmt-lem-duhamel"></a>
<div id="lem:duhamel" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:doubledduhamel"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Two-source Duhamel identity). *For the comparison sources just defined, $$\label{eq:doubledduhamel}
 \langle \mu_+,h\rangle
 =\int\langle s^+(ds),\bar b_s\rangle
  +\iint_{s\prec t}
          \langle T_{t,s}s^+(ds),s^-(dt)\rangle.$$ In the second integral the reverse sources are indexed by forward physical time. The order $s\prec t$ means that the forward insertion slice lies before the reverse insertion slice. Thus $T_{t,s}$ runs from after the first operation to before the later operation when both sources are atomic. There is no term pairing the forward and reverse sources of the same operation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-duhamel"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-duhamel</summary>

*Proof.* Here is the exact telescoping calculation for instantaneous factors; it also fixes the endpoint convention in the integrals. Let $K_j:V_{j-1}\to V_j$, $1\le j\le n$, be the chronological factors between counting spaces, and write $a_j,b_j$ for comparison values at those slices, with $a_0=b_n=h$. In this calculation $b_j$ is indexed in forward order. Put $$p_j=K_j a_{j-1}-a_j,\qquad
 q_j=K_j^*b_j-b_{j-1},\qquad
 P_{r,l}=K_r\cdots K_{l+1}.$$ The forward error is $\sum_jP_{n,j}p_j$. Reverse telescoping gives $$P_{n,j}^*h=b_j+\sum_{k>j}P_{k-1,j}^*q_k.$$ Their pairing is consequently $$\sum_j\langle p_j,b_j\rangle
       +\sum_{j<k}\langle P_{k-1,j}p_j,q_k\rangle.$$ The indices $j<k$ express that $p_j$ is inserted after its factor, whereas $q_j$ is inserted before that factor. On each intervening smooth interval the variation-of-constants formula gives the same identity with time integrals. Composing these formulas with the finite telescoping calculation proves <a href="#eq:doubledduhamel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:doubledduhamel">[eq:doubledduhamel]</a>. Two continuous source measures have no diagonal mass; atomic terms retain the strict slice order above. In particular the forward terminal seam source pairs directly with $h$ in the first sum. The reverse seam source lies before that operation and pairs only with earlier forward sources, through kernels ending before the seam. No kernel in this pairing crosses that seam twice. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:scalar"></a>
### The scalar error at the trial potential

The first error in the capacity comparison is the pairing of the propagated source with the trial potential itself. We estimate it using Lemma <a href="#lem:duhamel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duhamel">18</a>. Its first term benefits from signed cancellation on isolated strands. Its second term contains two sources, and heat-kernel decay and spatial localization make their joint contribution smaller. All expectations below use the actual pin law $\mathbb P_P$.

<a id="stmt-prop-scalar"></a>
<div id="prop:scalar" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (Scalar estimate). *With the scales of <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a>, under the interiority and pin assumptions of Section <a href="#sec:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profiles">5</a>, <a id="eq:scalarclaim"></a>$$\label{eq:scalarclaim}
 \mathbb E_P\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G}|\langle \mu_+,h\rangle|\bigr]
                         =o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ The error is uniform in the finite cube, the deterministic pin set, and the injection slot.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A single source paired with the free profile

<a id="eq:scalarweightintegral"></a>
For forward time $s$ put $$d=\sqrt{1+s},\qquad d'=\sqrt{1+\beta-s}.$$ The reverse profile $\bar b_s$ has value bound $C(G+P_{d'})$. The basic weight integral is $$\label{eq:scalarweightintegral}
 \int_0^\beta\sum_{r_z\le4R}
       (W+W_d)(z)(G+P_{d'})(z)\,ds
                  \le C\beta^{-1}(1+\log\beta).$$ To verify it, first sum the smooth product by radial shells: $$\sum_{r_z\le4R}W(z)G(z)
 \le C\beta^{-2}
       \left(1+\int_0^{4R}\frac{r^2\,dr}{(D_*+r)^3}\right)
 \le C\beta^{-2}(1+\log\beta).$$ For $s\le\beta/2$, Lemma <a href="#lem:freebounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:freebounds">14</a> gives $P_{d'}\le CG$, and $$\sum_z W_d(z)G(z)
 \le \sup G\sum_z W_d(z)\le C\beta^{-3/2}d^{-1}.$$ Integration over this half-period costs at most $C\beta^{-1}$. For $s\ge\beta/2$, use $W_d\le CW$ and $\sum_zP_{d'}(z)\le C$, so the remaining product costs at most $C\sup W=C\beta^{-2}$ per unit time. This proves <a href="#eq:scalarweightintegral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalarweightintegral">[eq:scalarweightintegral]</a>.

<a id="eq:nonisolatedprobability"></a>
Consider first the blocks contained in $[\beta^{1/4},\beta-\beta^{1/4}]$. On each such block $d,d'\ge\beta^{1/8}$ and their weights are comparable over the block and over distances $CL$. At a deterministic initial slice, Proposition <a href="#prop:snapshot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:snapshot">8</a> bounds the probability of an obstacle at a prescribed slot by $C\lambda$. If it is not isolated, a second distinct obstacle lies within $c_2L$, whence the same proposition and a union bound give $$\label{eq:nonisolatedprobability}
 \mathbb P_P(\text{the prescribed slot is an obstacle and is not isolated})
                       \le CL^3\lambda^2.$$ Here and below fixed slot multiplicities are included in constants.

For a nonisolated strand starting over $z$, the total variation bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">16</a> bounds its scalar contribution by $$CL(W+W_d)(z)(G+P_{d'})(z).$$ For an isolated strand subtract from the testing profile $\bar b$ its value at the strand’s initial site and time, at any one slot. The constant value pairs with the signed source, whose bound is $CL^{3/4}(W+W_d)(z)$. The remaining variation of the testing profile is at most <a id="eq:profilevariation"></a>$$\label{eq:profilevariation}
 CL(W+W_{d'})(z)
 \le C L\beta^{-1/8}(G+P_{d'})(z).$$ Spatial variation follows from the difference bounds over distance $CL$; time variation over the block follows from the time derivative bounds. The last inequality uses $W\le G/D_*$ and $W_{d'}=P_{d'}/d'$.

<a id="eq:centralfirstterm"></a>
Take expectations, use <a href="#eq:nonisolatedprobability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nonisolatedprobability">[eq:nonisolatedprobability]</a>, and sum the blocks. Their lengths are comparable to $L$, so their weight sums are bounded by $L^{-1}$ times <a href="#eq:scalarweightintegral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalarweightintegral">[eq:scalarweightintegral]</a>. The contribution of all these blocks is at most $$\label{eq:centralfirstterm}
 C\beta^{-1}(1+\log\beta)
   \left[L^3\lambda^2
          +\lambda\bigl(L^{-1/4}+L\beta^{-1/8}\bigr)\right]
                         =o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ The three powers of $\beta$ are respectively $-4+3\delta$, $-5/2-\delta/4$, and $-21/8+\delta$. The middle term is the one for which signed cancellation is needed.

The remaining blocks lie within $2\beta^{1/4}$ of an endpoint, for large $\beta$, because $L\ll\beta^{1/4}$. Use the bin total variation bound from Lemma <a href="#lem:sourcebins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sourcebins">17</a>, without cancellation. Weights at times and sites within a bin change by at most $H_\beta$, so the expected contribution is bounded by $H_\beta\lambda$ times the integral in <a href="#eq:scalarweightintegral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalarweightintegral">[eq:scalarweightintegral]</a> restricted to these endpoint intervals. On the first interval the preceding estimates give $$C\left[\beta^{1/4}\beta^{-2}(1+\log\beta)
       +\beta^{-3/2}\int_0^{2\beta^{1/4}}
                              (1+s)^{-1/2}\,ds\right]
 \le C\left[\beta^{-7/4}(1+\log\beta)+\beta^{-11/8}\right].$$ The last interval costs at most $C\beta^{-7/4}(1+\log\beta)$. Multiplication by $H_\beta\lambda$ gives powers at most $-23/8$ and $-13/4$, both strictly below $-5/2$.

<a id="eq:scalarbackground"></a>
The cutoff background is estimated separately. On its annulus the reverse profile has size at most $C/(\beta R)$, so $$\label{eq:scalarbackground}
 \left|\int\langle \gamma_s|_{H_s},\bar b_s\rangle\,ds\right|
 \le C\beta R^3 b_*\frac1{\beta R}
 =\frac C{\beta R}=o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ We have therefore bounded the first term of <a href="#eq:doubledduhamel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:doubledduhamel">[eq:doubledduhamel]</a>. It remains to control the interaction between its two source collections.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Snapshot moments for spatial boxes

The source locations at two different times are correlated. The following consequence of the snapshot estimate controls their pairing without an independence assumption.

<a id="stmt-lem-boxmoment"></a>
<div id="lem:boxmoment" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:boxmoment"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Box moment bound). *Partition the sites into cubes $Q$ of side comparable to $F\ge1$. At any deterministic slice let $\mathcal O$ be its obstacle slots, and let $w$ be a nonnegative deterministic site weight of finite support. With slot multiplicity in the sums, set $A_Q=\sum_{(z,a)\in\mathcal O,\ z\in Q}w(z)$. Then $$\label{eq:boxmoment}
 \left(\mathbb E_P\sum_Q A_Q^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le C\left[
  \sqrt\lambda\,\|w\|_2
  +\lambda\left\{\sum_Q
                     \left(\sum_{z\in Q}w(z)\right)^2\right\}^{1/2}
                 \right].$$ If two such sums are taken at different deterministic slices, the expected sum of products over pairs of anchors at distance at most $CF$ is bounded by a constant times the product of their respective right sides in <a href="#eq:boxmoment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boxmoment">[eq:boxmoment]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-boxmoment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-boxmoment</summary>

*Proof.* Expand $\sum_Q A_Q^2$. Terms using the same slot are bounded by $C\lambda\|w\|_2^2$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:snapshot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:snapshot">8</a>. Terms using two distinct slots, including two slots at the same site, are bounded by $C\lambda^2\sum_Q(\sum_{z\in Q}w(z))^2$. Taking a square root proves <a href="#eq:boxmoment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boxmoment">[eq:boxmoment]</a>. At a seam the same bound holds on either side: each $A_Q$ depends only on the number of obstacle slots at each site, and these numbers are preserved by the seam.

Distance at most $CF$ restricts the two anchors to cubes whose indices differ by one of a bounded number of offsets. For each offset, Cauchy–Schwarz in the product of the picture probability space and the cube index set gives $$\mathbb E_P\sum_Q A_Q A'_{Q+v}
 \le\left(\mathbb E_P\sum_Q A_Q^2\right)^{1/2}
      \left(\mathbb E_P\sum_Q (A'_Q)^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Sum the bounded number of offsets. Both factors use their own snapshot estimate, so no joint law at the two times is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:JB"></a>
In the applications, the weights are restricted to the deterministic anchor region $B_{4R}(x)$. We bound their norms by the corresponding unrestricted envelopes. For these weights, denote the resulting bounds by $$\label{eq:JB}
 \begin{aligned}
 J(d)&=C\bigl(\beta^{-3/4}d^{-5/2}
                         +\beta^{-3/2}d^{-1}\bigr),
       &&w=W_d,\\
 B(F)&=C\bigl(\beta^{-2}+\beta^{-5/2}F\bigr),
       &&w=W.
 \end{aligned}$$ To check the first line, direct shell sums give $\|W_d\|_2\le Cd^{-5/2}$ and $\|W_d\|_1\le Cd^{-1}$; the square root of the squared box sums is no greater than the total weight. For the second line, $\|W\|_2\le C\beta^{-1}D_*^{-1/2}$. It remains to bound the squared box sums by $C\beta^{-2}F^2$. Use the larger envelope $C\beta^{-1}(1+r_z)^{-2}$. The bounded number of cubes within distance $CF$ of $x$ each have weight sum at most $CF/\beta$. At distance comparable to $nF$, each cube has weight at most $C\beta^{-1}F n^{-2}$ and there are $O(n^2)$ cubes in such a shell. Summing their squares over $n\ge1$ gives $C\beta^{-2}F^2\sum_{n\ge1}n^{-2}$, as claimed.

<a id="eq:Jsum"></a>
The time sum of $J$ over the bins of either traversal satisfies $$\label{eq:Jsum}
 \sum_{\mathrm{bins}}J\bigl(\sqrt{1+s_{\mathrm{bin}}}\bigr)
                           \le C\beta^{-3/4}.$$ Indeed the bin lengths are bounded above and below, so $\sum(1+s_{\mathrm{bin}})^{-5/4}\le C$ and $\sum(1+s_{\mathrm{bin}})^{-1/2}\le C\sqrt\beta$. The latter gives $C\beta^{-1}$, which is smaller than the right side of <a href="#eq:Jsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Jsum">[eq:Jsum]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Two sources separated in time

First remove interactions involving cutoff background. Counting mass and supremum contractions of the actual kernels imply that two background sources cost at most $$C\beta^2b_*^2R^3=CR^{-3}.$$ For one background source, integrate its time variable and use its supremum bound. The resulting cost is at most $C\beta b_*$ times the total variation of the other, obstacle, source collection. Lemmas <a href="#lem:sourcebins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sourcebins">17</a> and <a href="#lem:boxmoment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxmoment">20</a>, or simply the one-point snapshot bound, give <a id="eq:expectedsourcevariation"></a>$$\label{eq:expectedsourcevariation}
 \mathbb E_P\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G}
                \int\|s^{\pm}_{\mathrm{obs}}\|_1\right]
                  \le H_\beta\lambda(R+\sqrt\beta).$$ For the smooth weight, use $\sum_{r_z\le4R}W(z)\le C\beta^{-1}R$ and $O(\beta)$ bins. For the point weight use $\sum_zW_d(z)\le C/d$ and sum in time. Thus all terms with background are $o(\beta^{-5/2})$.

We can now assume both sources are obstacle sources. Group their bin pairs by dyadic scales $T\in\{1,2,4,\ldots\}$, up to a constant times $\beta$, according to $1+t-s\asymp T$. The scale $T=1$ also includes a bounded number of neighboring and identical bins. Equivalently one may group by bin indices; the bin length bounds preserve comparability away from this first scale. For a fixed bin there are $O(T)$ partner bins at scale $T$.

<a id="eq:scalarinteractionradius"></a>
First replace each intervening propagator by its version killed on exiting $B_{R_*}(x)$. Every source starts in $B_{R+2}(x)$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">12</a> bounds the discarded mass by a rapidly small quantity, uniformly on $\mathcal G$. The source supports and total variations are bounded by fixed powers of $\beta$, by <a href="#eq:backgroundbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:backgroundbound">[eq:backgroundbound]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:sourcebins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sourcebins">17</a>; hence the aggregate loss in the double pairing is still rapidly small. Lemma <a href="#lem:heatbound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heatbound">11</a> now bounds each entry of the killed kernel by $CT^{-3/2}$. The displacement assertion in Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">12</a> further permits us, with another rapidly small aggregate error, to retain only anchor sites at distance at most $$\label{eq:scalarinteractionradius}
 F=F(T):=C(1+T/L)L^{3/4}.$$ The $C\log\beta$ shifts from the bin anchors to insertion sites are absorbed by $L^{3/4}$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:boxmoment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxmoment">20</a> with boxes of side comparable to $F(T)$. The two bin total variations contribute only $H_\beta$ times their anchor weights. We treat the three possible weight products in turn.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Two smooth weights.

<a id="eq:WWsum"></a>
There are $O(\beta)$ first bins and $O(T)$ partners per first bin. Their contribution at scale $T$ is at most <a id="eq:WWscale"></a>$$\label{eq:WWscale}
 H_\beta\beta T^{-1/2}B(F)^2
 \le H_\beta\left[\beta^{-3}T^{-1/2}
                        +\beta^{-4}T^{-1/2}F^2\right].$$ Since $F^2\le C(L^{3/2}+T^2L^{-1/2})$, the dyadic sum is at most $$\label{eq:WWsum}
 H_\beta\left[
       \beta^{-3}+\beta^{-4}L^{3/2}
                       +\beta^{-5/2}L^{-1/2}\right]
                         =o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ Here $\sum_TT^{-1/2}\le C$ and $\sum_{T\le C\beta}T^{3/2}\le C\beta^{3/2}$. The final term explains why a displacement bound smaller than $T$ is needed in <a href="#eq:scalarinteractionradius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalarinteractionradius">[eq:scalarinteractionradius]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### One smooth and one point weight.

<a id="eq:WPsum"></a>
Sum the point weight over its own elapsed-time bins using <a href="#eq:Jsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Jsum">[eq:Jsum]</a>; each such bin has $O(T)$ smooth partners. The scale-$T$ bound is $$H_\beta T^{-1/2}B(F)\beta^{-3/4}.$$ Inserting <a href="#eq:JB" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:JB">[eq:JB]</a> and <a href="#eq:scalarinteractionradius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalarinteractionradius">[eq:scalarinteractionradius]</a>, and summing dyadically, gives at most $$\label{eq:WPsum}
 H_\beta\left[
  \beta^{-11/4}+\beta^{-13/4}L^{3/4}
                         +\beta^{-11/4}L^{-1/4}\right]
                           =o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ This time the growing sum is $\sum_{T\le C\beta}T^{1/2}\le C\sqrt\beta$. The calculation applies whichever direction supplies the point weight, because <a href="#eq:Jsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Jsum">[eq:Jsum]</a> holds in either elapsed-time order.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### Two point weights.

When $t-s\le\beta/2$, their elapsed profile times satisfy $$s+(\beta-t)=\beta-(t-s)\ge\beta/2.$$ At least one is therefore at least $\beta/4$, and its point weight is bounded by $CW$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:freebounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:freebounds">14</a>. These pairs are already covered by <a href="#eq:WPsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:WPsum">[eq:WPsum]</a>. The remaining pairs have separation comparable to $\beta$. Use the entry bound $C\beta^{-3/2}$ and the unrestricted two snapshot pairing from Lemma <a href="#lem:boxmoment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxmoment">20</a>, with a single box containing the source support. Summing both time variables by <a href="#eq:Jsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Jsum">[eq:Jsum]</a> costs at most <a id="eq:PPsum"></a>$$\label{eq:PPsum}
 H_\beta\beta^{-3/2}
       \left(\sum_{\mathrm{bins}}J(d)\right)^2
                   \le H_\beta\beta^{-3}.$$

This finishes the estimate of the second term in <a href="#eq:doubledduhamel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:doubledduhamel">[eq:doubledduhamel]</a>. To conclude, the smallest power gains in the entire scalar calculation are $L^{-1/4}=\beta^{-\delta/4}$ in <a href="#eq:centralfirstterm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centralfirstterm">[eq:centralfirstterm]</a>, $\beta^{-\delta/4}$ in <a href="#eq:scalarbackground" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalarbackground">[eq:scalarbackground]</a>, and $L^{-1/2}=\beta^{-\delta/2}$ in <a href="#eq:WWsum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:WWsum">[eq:WWsum]</a>. Each dominates every fixed logarithmic power because $\delta=1/200>0$. All other displayed terms have larger power margins. Taking the expected absolute value in Lemma <a href="#lem:duhamel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duhamel">18</a> proves Proposition <a href="#prop:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scalar">19</a>.

<a id="sec:energy"></a>
### Residuals in the dual energy norm

We now bound the two residuals from Lemma <a href="#lem:duhamel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duhamel">18</a> against an arbitrary test vector, using the one-period energy of that vector. The scalar estimate of Section <a href="#sec:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scalar">7</a> is not sufficient for this purpose: the test vector need not resemble the free potential. Instead, we represent propagated sources as divergences of flows and pair those flows with the gradient of the propagated test vector. For a group of sources associated with one obstacle strand, we first route the signed mass to a nearby slot. Only the resulting net mass must then be sent to infinity. The cancellation in Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">16</a> reduces this second cost.

<a id="stmt-prop-dualenergy"></a>
<div id="prop:dualenergy" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:dualenergy"></a>
<a id="eq:dualenergyexpectation"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Dual energy estimate). *Fix $S$, and use the scales in <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a>. Suppose that $B_{50R_*}(x)$ lies in the interior of the finite cube and that no point of $E$ lies above this ball. Let $\mathcal G$ be the good event of Proposition <a href="#prop:good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good">10</a>, and let $\mu_+,\mu_-$ be the terminal residuals in <a href="#eq:residualdefinition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residualdefinition">[eq:residualdefinition]</a>. There are nonnegative measurable functions $X_+,X_-$ of the exposure and a deterministic rapidly small function $\rho(\beta)$ such that, on $\mathcal G$, $$\label{eq:dualenergy}
 \lvert \langle \mu_d,y\rangle\rvert
 \le X_d\sqrt{e_y}+C_0\rho(\beta),
 \qquad d\in\{+,-\},$$ for every real cut vector $y$ that vanishes on the cut killing slots and satisfies $\lVert y\rVert_\infty\le C_0$. The functions $X_d$ do not depend on $y$ or $C_0$, and $$\label{eq:dualenergyexpectation}
 \mathbb E_P\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G}(X_+^2+X_-^2)\right]
 =o(\beta^{-5/2}).$$ All estimates are uniform over the finite cubes, deterministic pin sets, and injection slots satisfying the stated assumptions.*

</div>

We work in one direction at a time, writing $U_d=U$ for the forward direction and $U_d=U^*$ for the reverse direction. Time in the following argument is elapsed time in this chosen traversal; all instantaneous operations retain the ordering fixed in Sections <a href="#sec:loops" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:loops">2</a> and <a href="#sec:sources" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sources">6</a>. Write $T_{t,s}$ for its propagator from $s$ to $t$, and extend this notation periodically when $t>\beta$.

<a id="eq:energy-suffix"></a>
For a terminal vector $y$, the adjoint suffix $Y(t)=T_{\beta,t}^*y$ satisfies, by Lemma <a href="#lem:kernelenergy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernelenergy">4</a>, $$\label{eq:energy-suffix}
 \int_0^\beta\mathcal D_t(Y(t))\,dt\le e_y,
 \qquad
 \lVert y-U_d^*y\rVert_2^2\le2e_y,
 \qquad
 \lVert Y(t)\rVert_\infty\le C_0.$$ These bounds hold for signed vectors. In particular, they require neither a reversible one-period kernel nor time-reversal invariance of the exposure law.

<a id="eq:flowpairing-energy"></a>
At a fixed slice, a finite-energy flow $J$ with divergence $\nu$ gives $$\label{eq:flowpairing-energy}
 \lvert \langle \nu,Y\rangle\rvert
 =\left|\sum_eJ(e)\bigl(Y(e^-)-Y(e^+)\bigr)\right|
 \le\bigl(2\lVert J\rVert_2^2\mathcal D_t(Y)\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ Here $Y$ is extended by zero on the killed inner boundary and outside the cube. It has finite support, so summation by parts has no term at infinity, and boundary-crossing edges are already included in $\mathcal D_t$. Nonzero total mass is allowed: the flows in Lemma <a href="#lem:flowoverlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flowoverlap">13</a> carry that mass to infinity on the extended available-slot graph. This is the same flow-pairing principle as in <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[BlochLaw](#ref-BlochLaw), Section 3.3]; the block cancellation below provides the additional saving needed here.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Flows for the sources of one block

Divide the sources in the chosen direction into two collections. The first contains all cutoff sources, all obstacle sources from the smooth potential, and the point-profile obstacle sources whose elapsed times lie in $[\beta/2,\beta]$. The second contains the remaining point-profile obstacle sources, at elapsed times in $[0,\beta/2)$. We begin with the first collection. Its obstacle sources satisfy the block bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">16</a> with envelope $W$, and its cutoff sources have size at most $Cb_*$ per slot and unit time, where $$b_*=\beta^{-1}R^{-3}$$ is the bound in <a href="#eq:backgroundsource" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:backgroundsource">[eq:backgroundsource]</a>. Since $\beta/2$ is a block division, no block is truncated when the late point-profile sources are included.

Let $I_1,\ldots,I_n$ be the consecutive blocks in this direction, with lengths between $L$ and $2L$. Thus $n\le C\beta/L$. For each block $I_j$, let $\mathcal A_j$ be the obstacle slots at its starting slice whose sites lie in $B_{4R}(x)$, and use the same index for the strand starting at such a slot. Denote its starting site by $z_\alpha$. Set $$\theta_\alpha=
 \begin{cases}
 L^{-1/4},&\text{if this strand is isolated in }I_j,\\
 1,&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 Q_j=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal A_j}W(z_\alpha)^2,$$ and $$C_j=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha'\in\mathcal A_j}
 \frac{\theta_\alpha\theta_{\alpha'}
 W(z_\alpha)W(z_{\alpha'})}
 {1+|z_\alpha-z_{\alpha'}|_\infty}.$$ These are finite snapshot sums, with slot multiplicities retained.

<a id="stmt-lem-blockflow"></a>
<div id="lem:blockflow" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:blockflowbound"></a>
<a id="eq:blockflowexpectation"></a>
<a id="eq:lastblocknorm"></a>
**Lemma 22** (One-block flow bound). *For the first collection of sources, fix a block $I_j$ on $\mathcal G$. Propagate its sources to a fully regular slice $t$ of $I_{j+1}$, interpreting $I_{n+1}=\beta+I_1$ in a copy of the period. Their propagated signed mass differs in counting $\ell^1$ norm by a rapidly small amount from a localized mass $\nu_j(t)$, supported in $B_{6R}(x)$, which is the divergence of a flow of squared norm at most $$\label{eq:blockflowbound}
 F_j=C\left(L^6Q_j+L^2C_j+L^2b_*^2R^5\right).$$ The constant and the localization error are uniform in $j$ and $t$. Moreover, $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:blockflowexpectation}
 \mathbb E_P[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G}F_j]
 \le C\bigl[&L^6\lambda\beta^{-2}D_*^{-1}
 +L^2(L^{-1/2}\lambda^2+L^3\lambda^3)\beta^{-2}R
 +L^2b_*^2R^5\bigr].
\end{aligned}$$ If the sources of the final block are propagated only to the terminal cut, their mass differs by a rapidly small amount in $\ell^1$ from a mass $m_{\mathrm{loc}}$ satisfying $$\label{eq:lastblocknorm}
 \lVert m_{\mathrm{loc}}\rVert_2^2
 \le K_n:=CL^5(Q_n+b_*^2R^3).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-blockflow"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-blockflow</summary>

*Proof.* Every obstacle source in the block is associated with a strand starting within $B_{4R}(x)$. Sources of that strand are within $CL$ of its start, and have total variation at most $CLW(z_\alpha)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">12</a> permits their propagation to the next block to be killed outside a larger ball of radius $CL$ about $z_\alpha$, at a rapidly small relative loss of total variation. Apply this restriction separately to each strand. Apply the same restriction about each injection site to the cutoff sources. The elapsed propagation time is at most $4L$, also for the final block continued into the copied period.

These balls lie in the unpinned interior. Up to the first exit from its chosen ball, the propagation conserves counting mass: the local heat evolution, hole transports, and seam averages all do so, and there is no pin killing there. The localized propagation loses mass only at that exit. Its signed net mass therefore differs from the signed sum of the injected sources by at most their total variation times the rapidly small exit probability. All source variations and the number of injection sites are polynomially bounded in $\beta$ on $\mathcal G$. Their aggregate loss is consequently rapidly small, uniformly in the volume and in the averaging slice.

Fix a fully regular averaging slice. For each $\alpha$, choose one available representative slot $a_\alpha$ within a bounded distance of $z_\alpha$. Proposition <a href="#prop:geometryinputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:geometryinputs">7</a> provides this slot and paths from every slot carrying the localized mass to $a_\alpha$, of length and displacement at most $CL$. Erase loops from these paths. Routing a signed mass along them produces a flow whose divergence is that mass minus its net mass at $a_\alpha$. Its norm is at most the total variation times the largest path norm, hence its squared norm is bounded by $$CL\bigl(LW(z_\alpha)\bigr)^2=CL^3W(z_\alpha)^2.$$ At a given edge, only strands starting within $CL$ can contribute. There are at most $CL^3$ such starting slots, by deterministic counting and fixed slot multiplicity. Cauchy–Schwarz on each edge therefore bounds the squared norm of the sum of these local flows by <a id="eq:blocklocalflow"></a>$$\label{eq:blocklocalflow}
 CL^6Q_j.$$

Let $q_\alpha$ be the remaining net mass at $a_\alpha$. The cancellation in Lemma <a href="#lem:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cancellation">16</a>, and the preceding conservation observation, give $$|q_\alpha|\le CL\theta_\alpha W(z_\alpha).$$ Indeed the error from localization is rapidly small relative to $LW(z_\alpha)$, so for an isolated strand it is absorbed into $CL^{3/4}W(z_\alpha)$. Send $q_\alpha$ to infinity along the unit flow from $a_\alpha$. Bounded displacement of these representatives preserves the inverse-distance overlap estimate in Lemma <a href="#lem:flowoverlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flowoverlap">13</a>, up to a constant. The squared norm of the resulting sum of flows is thus at most <a id="eq:blocknetflow"></a>$$\label{eq:blocknetflow}
 C\sum_{\alpha,\alpha'}
 \frac{|q_\alpha q_{\alpha'}|}
 {1+|z_\alpha-z_{\alpha'}|_\infty}
 \le CL^2C_j.$$ This is the only part of the obstacle flow that carries mass over arbitrarily large distances, and it is the part on which isolated-strand cancellation saves a factor $L^{-1/2}$ after squaring.

For the cutoff sources, each injection site contributes total variation at most $CLb_*$ in the block. Send their localized descendants directly to infinity using Lemma <a href="#lem:flowoverlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flowoverlap">13</a>. If two injection sites are within $CL$, use the constant overlap bound. There are at most $CR^3L^3$ such ordered pairs. For sites farther apart, the descendants are still separated by a constant multiple of their starting distance, after increasing the threshold $CL$. The inverse-distance sum over a ball of radius $CR$ is $CR^5$. Their total squared flow norm is at most <a id="eq:blockcutoffflow"></a>$$\label{eq:blockcutoffflow}
 CL^2b_*^2(R^3L^3+R^5)\le CL^2b_*^2R^5.$$ Adding the three flows and using the squared triangle inequality proves <a href="#eq:blockflowbound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:blockflowbound">[eq:blockflowbound]</a>. Every source of these flows is within $B_{6R}(x)$, since $L=o(R)$.

<a id="eq:weightenergysums"></a>
We next average the bound over the exposure. Radial shells give $$\label{eq:weightenergysums}
 \sum_{|z-x|_\infty\le4R}W(z)^2
 \le C\beta^{-2}D_*^{-1},
 \qquad
 \sum_{|z-x|_\infty,|z'-x|_\infty\le4R}
 \frac{W(z)W(z')}{1+|z-z'|_\infty}
 \le C\beta^{-2}R.$$ For the second estimate one may enlarge $W(z)$ to $\beta^{-1}(1+|z-x|_\infty)^{-2}$. Two comparable dyadic shells of radius $b$ contribute at most $C\beta^{-2}b$: for each site in one shell, the inverse-distance sum over the other is at most $Cb^2$. Shells of radii $c\le b/2$ contribute at most $C\beta^{-2}c$. Summing first over $c$ and then over the dyadic values of $b\le CR$ proves the estimate without a logarithmic factor.

Proposition <a href="#prop:snapshot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:snapshot">8</a> bounds the expectation of $Q_j$ by $C\lambda\sum W^2$. To estimate $C_j$, first take pairs whose starting sites are at distance at most $C_1L$, where $C_1$ is a sufficiently large fixed constant compared with the isolation radius. Charge only one obstacle, with probability $C\lambda$, and allow $CL^3$ possible second slots. The envelope $W$ is comparable at these distances because $L=o(D_*)$. This portion of $L^2C_j$ is bounded in expectation by $$CL^5\lambda\sum W^2,$$ which is absorbed into the $L^6$ term in <a href="#eq:blockflowexpectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:blockflowexpectation">[eq:blockflowexpectation]</a>.

For a more distant pair, the two starting slots are distinct. If both are isolated, their occupation probability is at most $C\lambda^2$, and $\theta_\alpha\theta_{\alpha'}=L^{-1/2}$. If at least one is not isolated, a third distinct obstacle slot must lie within distance $c_2L$ of that member. The separation ensures that this third slot is different from the other member of the pair. A union bound over its $CL^3$ possible positions and the three-point snapshot bound give probability at most $CL^3\lambda^3$. Applying <a href="#eq:weightenergysums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weightenergysums">[eq:weightenergysums]</a> proves <a href="#eq:blockflowexpectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:blockflowexpectation">[eq:blockflowexpectation]</a>. Only one-time snapshot probabilities have been used; no independence between strands or between blocks is needed.

Finally, at the terminal cut, the localized mass from one strand has $\ell^2$ norm at most its variation $CLW(z_\alpha)$. The mass from one cutoff injection site has norm at most $CLb_*$. Their supports have overlap at most $CL^3$. Cauchy–Schwarz at each slot proves <a href="#eq:lastblocknorm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lastblocknorm">[eq:lastblocknorm]</a>. The displayed bounds use measurable finite snapshot sums; choosing the flows measurably is unnecessary. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Averaging over the next block

The flow bound controls a block at any fully regular slice of its successor. We now use these choices of slice to share a single Dirichlet energy budget among the blocks. The final block requires a separate step, because its successor lies beyond the terminal cut.

<a id="eq:lastblockcorrection"></a>
Let $m_j$ be the terminal mass produced by the first collection of sources in $I_j$. For $j<n$, at a slice $t\in I_{j+1}$ the block pairing is the propagated source mass paired with $Y(t)=T_{\beta,t}^*y$. Lemma <a href="#lem:blockflow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:blockflow">22</a> and <a href="#eq:flowpairing-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flowpairing-energy">[eq:flowpairing-energy]</a> therefore apply directly. For the final block, put $m=m_n$ and use the exact identity <a id="eq:lastblockcopy"></a>$$\label{eq:lastblockcopy}
 \langle m,y\rangle
 =\langle m,y-U_d^*y\rangle+\langle m,U_d^*y\rangle.$$ The second term equals $\langle U_dm,y\rangle$. It is represented by continuing the source mass through a copy of the period, with terminal test vector $y$ at time $2\beta$. At its averaging slice $t\in\beta+I_1$, the test vector is consequently $T_{2\beta,t}^*y$. The localized norm bound and <a href="#eq:energy-suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-suffix">[eq:energy-suffix]</a> control the first term: $$\label{eq:lastblockcorrection}
 \lvert \langle m,y-U_d^*y\rangle\rvert
 \le \sqrt{2K_ne_y}+C_0\rho(\beta).$$ The localization error here is estimated in $\ell^1$, using $\lVert y-U_d^*y\rVert_\infty\le2C_0$. Figure <a href="#fig:period-averaging" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:period-averaging">2</a> shows the ordinary averaging windows and the window used for this continued final block.

<figure id="fig:period-averaging">

<figcaption>Averaging the source mass from one block in the next block. The last block uses the first block of a copied period. The displayed identity separates the pairing of its terminal mass $m$ into a term controlled by contraction energy and a term whose propagation can be split at the copied averaging slices. The averaging windows are disjoint modulo one period, so their Dirichlet energies are summed with a constant total cost. Block widths are schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-period-averaging"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:period-averaging`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.9cm,y=0.9cm,>=Stealth,
 every node/.style={font=\small}]
 \definecolor{flowblue}{RGB}{25,108,105}
 \foreach \a/\b/\lab in {0/1.3/I_1,2.5/3.8/I_j,3.8/5.1/I_{j+1},6.4/7.7/I_n}
  {\draw[fill=gray!7] (\a,0) rectangle (\b,0.75);
   \node at ({(\a+\b)/2},0.375) {$\lab$};}
 \node at (1.9,0.375) {$\cdots$};
 \node at (5.75,0.375) {$\cdots$};
 \draw[fill=flowblue!10] (7.7,0) rectangle (9.5,0.75);
 \node at (8.6,0.375) {$I_1+\beta$};
 \node at (10.05,0.375) {$\cdots$};
 \draw[->] (-0.25,-0.25)--(10.65,-0.25) node[right] {$t$};
 \draw (0,-0.2)--(0,-0.3) node[below] {$0$};
 \draw (7.7,-0.2)--(7.7,-0.3) node[below] {$\beta$};
 \draw[dashed] (7.7,-0.05)--(7.7,2.1);
 \draw[->,thick,flowblue] (3.15,0.9) to[out=70,in=110] (4.45,0.9);
 \node[align=center,text=flowblue] at (3.8,1.9) {propagate, then average\\on regular slices};
 \draw[->,thick,flowblue] (7.05,0.9) to[out=70,in=110] (8.6,0.9);
 \node[align=center,text=flowblue] at (8.5,2.55) {continue through\\the next period};
 \node[align=center] at (5.1,-1.3)
```
